# Decisions

The Phase 0 decision record. Each entry says what was decided, when, and what it
changes. Background for every item is in [`plan.md`](plan.md), which was written
before some of these decisions and assumes an EU company; where they differ,
this file wins.

## Settled

| # | Decision | Date | Consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | **A Norwegian company shipping from Norway.** | 2026-09-23 | Norway is the home market. Sales to Sweden and Denmark are exports into the EU, with EU import VAT and customs on every parcel (see below). Replaces the earlier assumption of an EU company. |
| D2 | **Launch in Norway, Sweden and Denmark.** | 2026-09-23 | These three markets are active (NOK, SEK, DKK; nb-NO, sv-SE, da-DK). The other 25 exist but are inactive; Germany was dropped from the launch list and can be switched back on in one line. |
| D3 | **Payments: Stripe.** Checkout Sessions with the Payment Element, Stripe Tax for VAT. | 2026-09-23 | Keeps card data off our servers (PCI DSS SAQ A). Local methods are configured per market in Stripe. `products.tax_code` holds the Stripe Tax code. |
| D4 | **Commerce core built from scratch**, not on an open-source platform. | 2026-09-23 | We own catalogue, pricing, carts, orders, returns and promotions. |
| D5 | **Changes go straight to `main`**, which deploys to production. | 2026-09-23 | Fine while the site is a placeholder. Revisit before launch: switch to pull requests with preview deployments. |
| D6 | **Region: Ireland.** Supabase `eu-west-1`; Vercel functions pinned to `dub1`. | 2026-09-23 | Both in the EU/EEA; functions sit next to the database. |
| D7 | **One Next.js app**, not a monorepo. | 2026-09-23 | Split into packages when a second deployable (such as the MCP server in Phase 4) needs shared domain code. |
| D8 | **Commerce data in a private `commerce` schema**, reached only by server code over a direct connection. | 2026-09-23 | Supabase's Data API never exposes it; row-level security is on with no policies as a backstop. |
| D9 | **Drizzle schema is the source of truth**; migrations are generated into `supabase/migrations` and applied to Supabase by Claude as they land. | 2026-09-23 | CI fails if schema and migrations disagree. |
| D10 | **Data residency: EU/EEA vendors wherever a comparable option exists.** A US processor only with a DPA and the EU–US Data Privacy Framework or standard contractual clauses. | 2026-09-23 | Default set by Claude. Every vendor is listed in [`residency-register.md`](residency-register.md) before it touches production data. |
| D11 | **Build to WCAG 2.2 AA.** | 2026-09-23 | Meets Norway's universal-design rules and the European Accessibility Act for Swedish and Danish customers. |
| D12 | **Kaizen Store is a multi-purpose template**, not tied to one product category. | 2026-09-23 | VAT comes from a Stripe Tax code per product (with a per-variant override); every goods withdrawal exclusion is available; producer responsibility schemes are tracked per product with registrations per market; `commerce.missing_registrations` shows the gaps per store. Variants carry a customs tariff (HS) code and country of origin for export declarations. |
| D13 | **No extra monitoring or analytics accounts until there is a need.** | 2026-09-23 | Errors: Vercel's built-in logs. Speed: Vercel Speed Insights. Traffic: Vercel Web Analytics (cookieless). Business events: our own tables in Postgres. Experiments: assignment in our own code. Sentry, PostHog and GrowthBook are optional add-ons, not prerequisites. |
| D14 | **No non-essential cookies at launch.** | 2026-09-23 | Cart and session cookies are strictly necessary and need no consent, so the store needs no cookie banner. Adding marketing pixels or cookie-based analytics later brings back the consent requirement. Default set by Claude. |
| D15 | **Payment settings belong to the store owner, in the store's admin.** API keys and webhook secrets per payment provider, test or live mode, and an on/off switch per payment method (per market) are edited in store settings, not in Vercel environment variables. | 2026-09-24 | Needs an admin area with sign-in (Phase 1c). Secrets are stored encrypted in the database with a key held only in the server environment, never returned to the browser once saved, and every change is logged. The only payment-related environment variable is that encryption key. |
| D16 | **Checkout on Stripe's hosted page, with each store's own Stripe account.** Kaizen places the order and holds the stock, then hands over to Stripe Checkout; a webhook (created automatically in the owner's Stripe account when they save their secret key) confirms payment. | 2026-09-24 | Card data never touches Kaizen (PCI DSS SAQ A). Orders are numbered per store from 1001. Stock is held for 35 minutes (the session's 30 plus a margin). VAT shown on orders uses each country's standard rate until Stripe Tax is connected; reduced rates are not applied yet (see R6). |
| D17 | **Stripe Connect: each store sells through its own connected Stripe account, created by Kaizen.** Replaces the pasted keys of D15 and D16. Configured as Stripe advises for Shopify-like platforms: Accounts v2, full Stripe Dashboard for the store, Stripe collects its fees from the store and carries negative balances, direct charges (the store is the seller), onboarding with Stripe's embedded components in the admin. | 2026-09-24 | Stores no longer paste Stripe keys, and Kaizen stores no store secrets: only Kaizen's own platform keys (`STRIPE_SECRET_KEY_TEST`/`_LIVE`, `STRIPE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY_TEST`/`_LIVE`) in Vercel. Two platform webhooks per mode, created from the platform page: payment events from stores' accounts, and Accounts v2 events that keep each store's status (`stripe_accounts`) current. Kaizen takes a platform-wide fee per sale as an application fee (`platform_settings.sale_fee_bps`, 0 until set); monthly plans come next with Stripe Billing. Payment methods are chosen in each store's Stripe Dashboard (dynamic payment methods), so the per-market switches of D15 are gone. Optional invoice PDF per order (Stripe Invoicing). VAT stays Kaizen's own calculation for now; Stripe Tax per store waits for tax advice on Norway-to-EU sales. Old per-store keys and webhooks stay in the database only so payments started before the switch still complete. |
| D18 | **Kaizen's plans for stores are managed in Kaizen and billed with Stripe Billing.** Platform admins create plans (tiers) with monthly and yearly prices per currency, excluding VAT, and a fee per sale; saving copies them to Stripe as Products and Prices. Admins start, change and cancel each store's plan and can give a store its own fee. | 2026-09-24 | The subscription lives on Kaizen's Stripe account with the store's own connected account as the customer (`customer_account`); Stripe emails an invoice each period, due in 14 days (`send_invoice`), with 25 % Norwegian VAT for stores in Norway and none for others (reverse charge). Store owners see their plan under Plan and open Stripe's customer portal for invoices and card details. A third platform webhook keeps `store_billing` current. The fee on a sale is the store's own fee, else its plan's while it is on the plan, else the platform default. Prices never change in place: a new amount is a new Stripe Price, and stores keep the old one until moved. Taking fees straight from a store's Stripe balance is left for when Stripe offers it outside preview. |
| D19 | **Owners choose their own plan, and can own several stores.** On a store's Plan page the owner picks a plan (monthly or yearly, in the store's currency) and pays by card through Stripe Checkout; later changes apply at once with proration, and they can cancel at the end of the period. An owner can create more stores from All stores, each a copy of the demo with its own Stripe account and plan. | 2026-09-24 | Plans still belong to a store, not a person. Platform admins can still put a store on an invoiced plan (D18). Only people who already own a store, or run the platform, can create stores (up to 10 each), so the beta stays invite-only (P3). `stores` is a reserved store address. |
| D20 | **Test payments need nothing from the store owner.** Kaizen sets up each store's test Stripe account itself, filled with Stripe's test values (date of birth 1901-01-01, address `address_full_match`, website `https://accessible.stripe.com`, test IBAN `NO9386011117947`) and Kaizen's acceptance of the service agreement, so test purchases land in the store's own test account straight away. Stripe's real identity checks happen only for the live account, before real money. New stores start with test payments on. | 2026-09-24 | Test accounts differ from live ones: no Stripe Dashboard, and Kaizen holds the fee and loss settings (Stripe allows this in test only because nothing real is at stake); live accounts stay as in D17. The account is created in the background when an owner opens the admin or Payments (the service agreement needs the owner's IP address); Stripe checks the test values within a minute or two, and a checkout in that window asks Stripe again rather than failing on an old status. Kaizen marks the accounts it made (`managed_by_kaizen`) and keeps Stripe's latest reasons (`requirements`, no personal data) so a stuck account can be explained. An older test account that waited for the owner's details is replaced; plans keep billing the account their subscription was started on. The storefront says when it is in test mode. |
| D21 | **Search and sharing are built in, for Kaizen and every store.** Every page has its title, description, canonical and hreflang links, Open Graph and X tags, and schema.org data: the store as an `OnlineStore` with its return policy, each market home as a `WebSite` and product list, each product as a `Product` (or a `ProductGroup` when variants differ by colour, size, material or pattern) with price, stock, shipping to the market, return policy and breadcrumbs. The site serves `robots.txt`, a sitemap index with one sitemap per open store (hreflang and pictures included), and `llms.txt` for Kaizen and for each store (terms, markets, shipping and every product with its price). Owners set their front page's title and description per language, a share picture, social profiles, AI crawler choices, extra crawler rules, their own llms.txt text and search console codes under Search; products get their own search title and description per language. Kaizen's own pages are set under Platform → Search. | 2026-09-24 | Everything is filled in from the store's data when the owner writes nothing; a page without a picture shares one Kaizen draws. Until stores have their own addresses (P2), crawlers read only the site's robots.txt, so each store's rules are added to it under the store's path, and the rules for everyone are repeated in each crawler's group (a crawler follows only its own group). AI assistants and AI search are welcome by default and can be turned off separately from AI training. Carts, order pages and the admin are left out; stores not yet opened, or hidden by their owner, are `noindex` and left out of sitemaps and llms.txt. The 14-day return policy states the legal right of withdrawal, with return costs on the shopper (the legal default) until stores can set their own terms (Phase 1d). A blog or other content types will use the same fields when they are built. |
| D22 | **Shoppers pay on Kaizen's own checkout page, with Stripe's payment form.** The cart's checkout button places the order as before and opens a Stripe Checkout Session with `ui_mode: "elements"` on the store's own Stripe account; the shopper then pays at `/{market}/checkout` on the store's site: Kaizen's order summary, then Stripe's express buttons (Apple Pay, Google Pay, Link, Klarna where offered), contact, delivery address and payment Elements, in the store's colours and the market's language. Paying leads to the order page as before. Stripe's hosted page stays as a platform-wide fallback (Platform → Stripe). | 2026-09-24 | Stripe's recommended way to build a custom checkout: the session still carries prices, shipping, Kaizen's fee, the invoice and the payment methods each store has turned on, and orders are still confirmed by Stripe's events, so nothing else changes. Card details go from the browser straight to Stripe. The session's client secret is kept with the payment so the page need not ask Stripe on every load; only the cart's own browser is shown it. Stripe needs each store's account to have the site's domain registered for Apple Pay, Google Pay, Link and Klarna; Kaizen does this at the first checkout on each domain and records it (`payment_domains`), and a failure only hides those methods. If the cart changes after the order was placed, or the 30 minutes run out, the page asks the shopper to continue again, which replaces the order. |
| D23 | **Every store's Stripe account asks for cards, Klarna, Link and MobilePay.** Kaizen requests these payment capabilities when it creates a store's account (test and live), and for older accounts in the background when the store's admin is opened or a checkout starts; it records what it asked for (`payment_methods_requested`). On the test accounts Kaizen makes, it also switches these on (with Apple Pay and Google Pay) in the account's display settings, which otherwise follow the platform's defaults for connected accounts (`payment_methods_shown`); owners of live accounts choose in their own Stripe Dashboard. Test accounts also carry Stripe's test Norwegian org number, which MobilePay asks for. Each order records the methods Stripe offered (`payment.started`), and each account keeps why a method is not on yet (`requirements`). | 2026-09-24 | Stripe then offers each method where it fits the shopper's country and currency (MobilePay in Denmark and Finland), and the store's own Stripe settings still decide. Swish and Vipps are in private preview at Stripe: Kaizen's platform account must be given access first, and Vipps is not yet available for Accounts v2; they are added to the list once possible. PayPal is not offered: Stripe does not allow it for platforms whose stores take direct charges on their own accounts. |
| D24 | **Every variant is either shipped or downloaded; a product sets the default.** As in Shopify (a per-variant "physical product" flag) and WooCommerce (Virtual and Downloadable), delivery is chosen per variant, so one product can sell a hardcover and an e-book; the editor asks once per product and offers per-variant choice behind a checkbox. Downloads have no stock, weight or customs details, need no shipping and no manufacturer (EU product-safety rules cover physical goods), and must have at least one file before they go on sale. Files are uploaded from the owner's browser straight to a private storage bucket (`digital-files`, one folder per store) and belong to every digital variant of the product or to one of them. When an order is paid, each digital line gets one personal link per file (`order_downloads`), limited by the product's downloads per file (5 by default) and days (30 by default), either of which can be off. The link counts a download in one statement and sends the shopper to a signed storage address that works for 60 seconds. Buying a download needs the shopper's tick at checkout: express consent to delivery straight away and acknowledgement that the right of withdrawal then ends (CRD art. 16(m); angrerettloven § 22 n). The time is kept on the order (`digital_consent_at`) and in its history; digital lines record the `digital_content` withdrawal exclusion and shipped lines never do. | 2026-09-24 | A basket of downloads only has no shipping, and the checkout form asks for no address. Download links show on the order page, which only the shopper has (it needs the Stripe session in its address); there is no order email yet, so it is the only place until emails exist. Files removed from a product stop new sales but keep working for earlier buyers. New stores copy the template's catalogue with each product's delivery, but not its files, so a download in the template arrives as a draft. Subscriptions (the next slice) will be purchase options on a product, as in Shopify's selling plans and WooCommerce's subscription options. |
| D25 | **Subscriptions are purchase options on a product, charged by Stripe Billing on the store's own account.** As Shopify's selling plans and WooCommerce's subscription options: a product gets up to five purchase options (every 1–52 weeks, 1–12 months or 1–3 years, with a subscriber's discount in whole percent), and can be sold only by subscription. Shoppers choose on the product page between buying once and an option; the prices follow. One checkout starts at most one subscription, so a cart holds one schedule (items bought once can join it). The checkout then runs in Stripe's subscription mode, with Kaizen's fee as `application_fee_percent`; the shopper ticks that they agree to the renewal amount and schedule, and that they can cancel any time. Kaizen keeps the subscription and what each renewal holds (`subscriptions`, `subscription_lines`), at the prices agreed at the start. Each paid renewal (`invoice.paid`, `subscription_cycle`) becomes a paid order: stock is drawn and downloads made as for any order, once per invoice. Stripe's subscription events keep the status, next renewal and cancellation in step. Shoppers cancel from a link on their order page, without an account (as easy as subscribing), effective at the end of the paid period, and can take it back; staff can also cancel at once. | 2026-09-24 | Stripe takes no shipping options in subscription mode, so shipping is a line: a subscription with something to ship pays shipping on every delivery, worked out on what each delivery holds (free above the store's threshold), and items bought once travel with the first delivery. Without such a subscription the usual rule applies. Options taken off a product are switched off, not deleted, so carts, orders and subscriptions keep pointing at them. Kaizen's existing webhook for stores' accounts is updated with the subscription events at the first subscription checkout, without replacing it. Stripe sends its own invoice for every charge. Not yet: trials, sign-up fees, pausing, skipping or swapping a delivery, changing quantities, a minimum number of renewals, renewal reminders by email (no emails yet), and Stripe's customer portal. |
| D26 | **Kaizen sends shoppers' emails in the store's name, through Resend (D32).** The sender is Kaizen's verified address with the store's name, and replies go to the store's contact address. Every email is written to `email_messages` before it is sent (who, why, subject, the email itself, and whether it was sent), so staff can read exactly what a shopper got: stores under Orders → Emails to customers, and each order's history; Kaizen under Platform → Emails. Emails are plain HTML with a text version, in the order's language, with the store's legal details at the foot. A key per event (such as `order-confirmation:{order}`) makes each email go once, however often a webhook arrives. | 2026-09-24 | Until `RESEND_API_KEY` and `EMAIL_FROM` are set in Vercel, emails are only recorded (`logged`), and nothing else waits on them. The sending domain must be verified in Resend (D32). Amazon SES was the first plan and was removed unused. The first emails: the order confirmation, for new orders and each subscription renewal, with the download and subscription links. Sign-in codes, shipping, refunds and subscription reminders follow. Stripe's own receipts can stay on as well (R7). |
| D27 | **Staff manage the whole order from its page.** Mark it sent with carrier and tracking number (Posten, Bring, PostNord, DHL, UPS build their tracking link; others take a pasted one), with an email to the customer; more parcels can follow (`shipments`). Refund any amount up to what is left, through Stripe on the store's own account, with Kaizen's fee on the refunded part returned to the store (`refund_application_fee`); choose which items go back in stock, with or without money back; the customer is emailed. Cancel a paid order before it is sent: everything left is refunded, all items go back in stock and download links stop. Correct the customer's email and delivery address, keep notes only staff see, print a packing slip in the customer's language, and send the confirmation again. The history shows every event and every email the customer got. | 2026-09-24 | Refunds of subscription orders refund that payment only; the subscription goes on until cancelled on its page. Orders cancelled after payment stay in the order list, marked "Cancelled and refunded"; a "To send" filter lists paid orders with something to ship. Refunds made directly in Stripe's Dashboard are not yet read back into Kaizen. Items go back to the store's first stock location. |
| D28 | **Shoppers get My account per store, signed in with an emailed code or a password they chose.** The code (six digits, ten minutes, five tries, at most five an hour, stored only as a hash) also proves the address, so every order and subscription placed with it joins the account, and later ones join when paid. After that, the customer can add a password (at least 12 characters, scrypt, ten wrong tries lock it for 15 minutes); codes keep working. Nothing on the sign-in page reveals whether an address has an account. The account shows orders (with tracking, downloads and refunds), subscriptions (managed on their own page), the customer's name, phone and address, the password, and deleting the account. A session is a random token in an httpOnly cookie for the store, of which only a hash is stored; it lasts 60 days. | 2026-09-24 | Guest checkout stays as it is. Deleting an account removes the customer's details, password and sessions; orders stay with the store, as bookkeeping law requires, without the link to an account. The account's address starts as the one the last order went to. Signing in by code needs email to be set up (D26); until then codes are only recorded, so shoppers cannot sign in on a store in production. Checkout does not yet prefill from the account. |
| D29 | **Purchase options can have a free trial, a sign-up fee and a commitment; subscribers pause, skip and change their subscription themselves, and are reminded before they are charged.** A free trial (up to 90 days) charges nothing for what renews until it ends (Stripe's `trial_period_days`), and its first delivery ships free; items bought once in the same checkout pay as usual. A sign-up fee is set per market and charged once with the first order, as its own line. A commitment (up to 24 payments, the first included) means a cancellation takes effect when the last committed payment's period ends (Stripe's `cancel_at`); it counts payments made, so pauses and skips move it. Pausing holds up to three deliveries at a time (Stripe's `pause_collection`, which voids the invoices due meanwhile); skipping is a pause of one; not during a trial or while a payment is failing. Changing contents swaps a line to another variant of the same product and delivery, or changes its quantity, or removes it (one must stay); a swapped line takes today's price with the option's discount, a kept one keeps its price; shipping is worked out again, and Stripe charges the new amounts from the next renewal, with nothing prorated. Every change is confirmed by email. A daily job emails a reminder three days before a trial ends and seven days before renewals of a month or longer, once per charge date. Staff can do all of this from the subscription's page, and also end a subscription at once. | 2026-09-24 | Weekly renewals get no reminder: each renewal's confirmation serves. Options with different trials cannot share a checkout, like options with different schedules. Content changes replace the Stripe subscription's items (products are reused by name). Commitment dates are worked out from Stripe's period end by the calendar, so a month-end renewal can end a few days early. The reminder job needs `CRON_SECRET` in Vercel (Vercel Cron sends it); without it, the route refuses every call and no reminders go out. Reminders, like every email, are only recorded until email is set up (D26). |
| D30 | **Every store has a header and footer its owner edits: a logo, a header menu and a footer menu, in each of the store's languages.** Kept on the store as one JSON value (`stores.navigation`), loaded and cached with the store, so pages make no extra query. A menu link goes to the front page, a product (named by handle, so copies of the template keep working), My account, the cart, or a web address (http(s), or a path in the store); at most 8 header and 12 footer links. A product link left without text takes the product's title. The header stays at the top and slides away while the shopper scrolls down, and comes back on the way up; it holds the logo (or name), the header menu on computers, a country chooser, My account and the cart with its count. On phones a Menu button opens a menu that slides in from the left (a modal dialog: focus stays in it; Escape, tapping outside, choosing a link or swiping it away closes it), and a bar fixed along the bottom (Home, Menu, My account, Cart) slides away while scrolling up, when the header returns, so only one shows. On product pages that bar becomes the price, a variant choice and Add to cart, following the purchase option chosen on the page. The footer shows the business details the law requires on every page, the footer menu and the countries. New stores start with the template's logo and menus. | 2026-09-24 | The logo is uploaded like product pictures (shrunk in the browser, up to 800 px) and shown up to 40 px high; PNG, JPEG or WebP, not SVG, which the browser cannot shrink. Menus are one level deep: there are no collections or content pages to group yet. The phone menu and bottom bar are each rendered in their own Suspense boundary: React counts everything outside boundaries towards a 12.8 kB budget, beyond which a page's content is sent in a block that needs JavaScript to show; keeping these enhancements out keeps product pages readable without it. The motion is off for shoppers who ask for reduced motion. |
| D31 | **Stores have discount codes for shoppers, and Kaizen has discount codes for stores' plans.** A store's code gives a percentage, an amount per market (in its currency) or free shipping; on everything or chosen products; with an optional minimum order per market, start and end (Norwegian time), a total number of uses, and one use per customer. Shoppers type it in the cart, which checks it against the basket exactly as checkout will and says why a code does not apply; checkout checks it again under a row lock, so two orders cannot take a code's last use. A code counts as used by every order that got it and is not cancelled, waiting for payment included; a cancelled checkout gives the use back. The discount is spread over the lines it applies to (a percentage per unit, an amount in proportion) and each line's VAT is on what is paid. Stripe gets it as a one-time coupon on the store's account for that checkout alone, for single payments and subscriptions alike; free shipping on a single payment is simply a lower shipping price. A percentage can also lower every renewal of a subscription: the subscriber's price itself is lowered, for good. Orders, emails, My account and the admin show the discount and the code. Kaizen's codes for plans are a percentage or an amount per plan currency, for the first payment, some months or for good, with an optional expiry and number of uses; each is a coupon and a promotion code in Kaizen's Stripe account (in every mode with keys), and Stripe applies it to the plan's invoices. Owners type one on their Plan page: it applies at once to a running plan, or is saved and used when they choose one (in Stripe Checkout or when an admin assigns a plan). Platform admins make and switch codes on the platform's Discounts page, and can put one on any store's plan. | 2026-09-24 | "Once per customer" needs the shopper signed in to My account: before payment Kaizen does not know who is buying otherwise. Any code can be changed or deleted at any time, used or not: orders keep the code's text and the amounts they got, so changing or deleting a code never changes an order (deleting only unlinks it). Stripe's coupons cannot change, so changing what a platform code gives retires its coupon and promotion code in Stripe and makes new ones under the same code; a plan that already has it keeps the old terms, and a store whose code was still waiting for a plan loses it when the code is deleted. Codes are case-insensitive and stored in capitals; store codes may use Æ, Ø and Å, Kaizen's codes only A–Z (Stripe). Stacking several codes on one order is not offered. Codes typed on Stripe's own checkout page are not offered either; codes are entered in Kaizen only, so Kaizen always knows which code a plan has. |
| D32 | **Shoppers can create an account with a password, on My account or at checkout; an email the store already knows chooses a new password instead. Emails go through Resend.** My account has two tabs, Sign in and Create account (name, email, password of at least 12 characters); a new account is signed in at once and gets a welcome email. At checkout, shoppers who are not signed in can tick Create an account and choose a password; it is kept (hashed) with the unpaid order, and the account opens when the order is paid, with the email, name and address they paid with, and that order. If the email already has an account, or paid orders, no account is made: the shopper is asked to choose a new password, which takes a six-digit code emailed to that address (the same rules as sign-in codes); the code proves the inbox is theirs, so all the email's orders then join the account and every other session ends. At checkout this is said on the order page, with the code form right there. An account made with a password starts with its email unproven: only orders placed while signed in join it, until the customer signs in with a code or chooses a new password, so registering someone else's email shows nothing of theirs. Emails are sent with Resend's API from a domain verified in Resend's EU region (Ireland), with each email's id in Kaizen as the idempotency key, so a retry never sends twice; a busy or failing Resend is tried again briefly, and anything else is marked failed in the email log. Resend reports deliveries, bounces and spam complaints to a signed webhook, and the email log shows them. | 2026-09-24 | Chosen by the owner for the easiest setup, price and API (Amazon SES, first planned in D26, was never configured and is removed). Resend is a US company: allowed by D10 with its DPA and standard contractual clauses; see the residency register. Saying that an email has shopped before tells whoever types it that the address has been used in the store; the owner asked for it, as larger shops do. Registering is not rate-limited per visitor yet. A password chosen at checkout is dropped once the order is paid, whatever the outcome. The order page signs the new account in once, within an hour of payment. |
| D33 | **Stores can email reminders about carts left at checkout, and Kaizen about plans left unpaid.** A store turns them on under Cart reminders and starts with three (after an hour, a day and three days), each editable: when it goes (30 minutes to 30 days after the email was typed), on or off, an optional discount code, and a subject, heading, message and button in each of the store's languages, beside a live preview and a test sent to the staff member. While they are on, checkout keeps the email of a shopper who is not signed in as soon as a valid address is typed, with the cart as ordered; right under the email field it says a reminder may come, with a link to say no (and to undo that). Each reminder shows the cart and its total, and has a button that puts the same cart back in the shopper's browser (with the step's code) and a link to stop reminders, with List-Unsubscribe one-click headers. At most one reminder per cart per run, the latest due, so a late run never sends several; each is claimed before it is sent and keyed, so none goes twice. Reminders stop when the cart is paid, when the same email buys anything, when the shopper opts out, and 30 days after capture; the email and cart are erased on opt-out and 60 days after capture. The admin shows the last 30 days (carts with an email, reminded, bought after a reminder, and those sales) and the latest carts. The same applies to Kaizen's own checkout, the plans stores pay for: under Platform → Plan reminders, platform admins turn on and edit reminders (in English, like the admin, with Kaizen's plan discount codes) to owners who went to Stripe to pay for a plan and did not finish. The owner is signed in, so the attempt is kept when they go to pay (the latest per store); the Plan page and Stripe's page say a reminder may come, and the Plan page and every reminder let the owner say no (for all of Kaizen's plan reminders). The reminder's button takes the owner straight back to paying for the same plan, with the code applied; reminders stop once the store is on a plan, however it got there. | D37 | **A function never leaves a database connection half-used.** The app talks to Postgres through node-postgres, which sends a query and its values in one message, one query at a time per connection. postgres.js, used before, sent a query with values in two halves without prepared statements and pipelined queries; through the transaction-mode pooler the second half could reach another Postgres connection once the pooler had handed the first back, so the query waited forever while a Postgres connection sat holding its first half. That was the main cause of pages hanging under load. Also, Vercel freezes a function instance between requests. Frozen in the middle of a query, it left the pooler's connection to Postgres waiting for the rest of it (seen: two such connections held for five minutes); enough of those and every query waits for a free connection, so pages hung on their loading placeholders until the pooler let go. Frozen with connections open, it woke up to dead ones. Now every query asks Vercel (through its `attachDatabasePool`) to keep the instance running until the query is done and its connections have been idle for five seconds and closed. As a safety net, Postgres ends any transaction of the app's role open for a minute, or idle inside a transaction for 30 seconds, so a connection is never held for long. The cached reads pages are built from try once more on a passing error (a timeout, a lost connection), so one hiccup does not fail a page or a build. | 2026-09-25 | After pages intermittently hung on "Loading …" in stores and the admin, and a build failed on a timed-out read. The app's transactions take milliseconds and call no outside service, so the limits never cut a real one. |

An order that starts a subscription is placed again only after the shopper agrees to its new renewal price, as when the cart changes. The cart keeps its own field. On the platform, the code field sits with the plans to choose from, and each plan shows what it costs with a code waiting for it and for how long; without a code saved, Stripe's payment page has its own field for Kaizen's codes, and a code typed there is recognised by its Stripe promotion code and recorded on the store's plan like any other. | 2026-09-25 | Asked for by the owner. Stripe checks a code typed on its page by the same rules (on, not expired, uses left, the plan's currency) as Kaizen. |

The banner offers Reject all and Accept all equally, plus a choice per category; the choice can be changed from the Cookies page, withdrawing deletes the cookies it covered, and visitors are asked again when the categories in use change (the version). | D60 | **Design themes for stores.** Every style setting of a storefront is a theme (`stores.theme`: the template it started from, the saved theme it came from, and its settings; `src/lib/theme.ts`): colour mode (the visitor's device, always light, always dark) with a light and a dark set of seven colours (background, surface, text, secondary text, lines, accent, text on accent), heading and body fonts (D59, now part of the theme), heading weight and capitals, filled or outlined buttons and their corners, corners of cards, pictures and fields, page width, the header's logo (left or centred) and background (like the page, surface, accent, or inverted in the text colour), and product cards (picture shape, plain/bordered/raised, left or centred). | D63 | **Businesses see prices without VAT; VAT is always charged.** A store sells to consumers, businesses or both (`stores.audience`, the Company page). In a store selling to both, each shopper is private or business, chosen with the header's switch or the optional first-visit question (`business_popup`) and kept in the necessary cookie `buyer_{storeId}`; the page's first script marks `<html data-buyer>` so prebuilt pages show each kind its prices (`<Price>` draws both, CSS shows one) and products (`products.audience`: everyone, private only, businesses only; cards carry `for-business`/`for-private`). Loading `/cart` itself, and every screen from 768 px, shows the cart page: the drawer reloads the address there. It works on stores' own hosts, whose rewrites the interception follows. The drawer is always a modal dialog, in the top layer over the header and bars; Next.js keeps a page left by a link hidden, which takes a dialog out of the top layer, so the drawer closes when hidden and opens again as a modal when shown. Stores choose per service or listing whether it is paid at booking, at the venue, or by a saved card charged later. | D66 | **Appointments are paid now, with a deposit, or at the venue, chosen per appointment; shoppers cancel and move within the appointment's rule; no-show fees are charged only when staff ask.** Each appointment says how it is paid (all now, the default; a deposit of 1–99 % now, 30 % by default; or all at the venue) and until how many hours before shoppers may cancel or move it themselves (24 by default). An order keeps its full total and VAT; what is left for the venue is its balance (`orders.balance_minor`, per line `order_lines.venue_minor`), which staff mark paid (card terminal, cash, other) from the order. Stripe is sent only what is due now: each line at that amount with any discount inside it, no coupon and no Stripe invoice (the store invoices the whole at the venue), Kaizen's fee on that amount. A deposit keeps the card (`setup_future_usage`, a Stripe customer on the store's account) so staff can charge a no-show fee (a percentage of the price, 0 by default, less the deposit). | D67 | **Stays and rentals are booked by whole nights or days over rooms and items; a cart line's quantity is the nights or days, so prices, discounts, VAT and deposits work as for any line.** Products of kind `stay` (nights in a room or home, accommodation VAT by default) and `rental` (days with an item) are booked over `booking_resources` of kind `unit` or `item` (a name and how many at once, no hours). Each has a check-in and check-out time (pick-up and return), a shortest and longest length (1–365), notice, how far ahead, and the payment and cancellation rules of D66. A stay of N nights runs from check-in on its first day to check-out N days later, so a guest may arrive the day another leaves; a rental of N days from pick-up on the first to return on the last. The product page shows four weeks of dates, the shopper picks the first and the last, and the cart line carries the check-in instant and the length; `commerce.hold_booking` holds the whole span at checkout, as for appointments. Shoppers cancel stays and rentals themselves within the rule but do not move them. | 2026-09-27 | Defaults chosen for the owner: one flat price per night or day (seasonal prices and a cleaning fee come in B3c), whichever room or item is free (no choice for the shopper), check-in 15:00 and check-out 11:00, rentals 09:00–17:00. B3 is built in parts: B3a (this), B3b (blocked dates and calendar sync with Airbnb and Booking.com), B3c (seasonal prices, cleaning fee). |
| D68 | **Rooms, items and staff are closed by blocks: dates the store sets, and events read from other sites' calendars; each room or item publishes its own taken days at a secret iCal address.** A block (`resource_blocks`) closes the whole resource whatever its capacity, and every booking check counts it: `busyOn()` for the dates and times offered, `commerce.resource_blocked()` under the lock in `hold_booking`, `move_booking` and a late payment. Calendars from Airbnb, Booking.com and the like (`calendar_feeds`) are read every 15 minutes from the five-minute cron, and on adding or asking; each read makes the feed's blocks what the file says (by the event's UID), and a feed that fails keeps its blocks and shows why. Whole-day events close a room from noon to noon, so the morning's guest leaves and the afternoon's arrives, and an item or a person from midnight to midnight. A block over an existing booking is allowed and shown as booked twice for the store to sort out. | 2026-09-27 | Defaults chosen for the owner (B3b). | D69 | **A rental's variants are each rented by whole days, half days or hours, and a resource is full only when its bookings at one moment reach its capacity.** `product_variants.rental_period` (`day`, `half_day`, `hour`) is set per variant in the product editor, so one product offers several ways to rent at their own prices. Whole days work as in D67. A half day is the morning (pick-up to the middle of the day, on a quarter hour) or the afternoon (from there to return), quantity 1. Hours start on the hour from pick-up and end by return time; the quantity is the hours. The product page asks for a day, then a half or a start time and how many hours (`rentalTimes()`), and a date shows as free when any of its halves or hours is. | D70 | **Stays and rentals are priced night by night through seasons, with a fee per booking; the booking is one line at its whole price.** A season (`booking_seasons`) recurs every year between two days (`MM-DD`, across the new year too, or all year), on chosen weekdays (a night counts as the day it starts), and changes the price by a percentage (−90 to +500); seasons that meet multiply, and each date's price is rounded to whole units. A fee per booking (`appointment_settings.booking_fee`, per market, VAT-inclusive, taxed as the product) is a stay's final cleaning or a rental's fee. The cart and the order hold a stay or rental as quantity 1 at its whole price (nights at their seasons' prices plus the fee), with the nights, days or hours on the booking (`CartLine.booking.count`, the parts in `booking.price`), so totals, discounts, VAT, deposits and refunds need nothing new; hours are priced by the date they fall on. | D71 | **A store can list stays and rentals for outside hosts: hosts sign in to an area of their own, are paid directly through their own Stripe account, and the store keeps a commission; the store is the platform operator for DAC7, and Kaizen prepares its report.** A host (`commerce.hosts`: their account, a display name, the store's commission in basis points, 15 % by default, and whether they are VAT registered) is added by the store's owners and signs in like staff, but is not a store member: `requireMember()` never admits them, and `requireHost()` gives them `/admin/hosting/{store}` with only their listings (`products.host_id`), their rooms and items (`booking_resources.host_id`) with calendars they keep, and the bookings of those. The store writes the listings; hosts do not edit them. A host who is not VAT registered sells without VAT (a trigger keeps their listings `exempt`). Payment (B4b): a booking with a host is a direct charge on the host's connected account (the host is the seller; `host_stripe_accounts`, which `connected_accounts` lists with the stores' own for webhooks, refunds and order pages), with Kaizen's fee plus the store's commission (a share of what is paid online, `orders.commission_minor`) as the application fee. Once paid, the commission is owed to the store (`host_commissions`, one per payment) and sent from Kaizen's balance as a transfer to the store's account, tried again from the five-minute cron until Stripe takes it (the fee must be in Kaizen's available balance, and the store's account is asked for the transfers capability on the first refusal); a refund returns the same share of it, by lowering it or reversing part of the transfer. A no-show fee charged to the card saved with a host's booking (D66) is charged on the host's account too, with the store's commission of it taken and sent the same way (kind `no_show`); hosts and the store see it next to the booking (hosts also see Kaizen's own fee on each payment, kept as `payments.kaizen_fee_minor` and less its refunded share), and the DAC7 report counts the fee as paid to the host and its commission as the store's. One checkout pays one host: a cart mixing hosts, or a host and the store, is refused (`host_mix`), and host listings are never subscriptions. Live, hosts open their own Stripe account (full Dashboard, Stripe's fees) with Stripe's embedded screens in their area; in test mode Kaizen makes it (D20). The store's invoices are not used for hosts' charges; the site's domain is registered on the host's account (`host_stripe_accounts.payment_domains`) as on the store's, so wallets, Link and Klarna show for their bookings too. A part paid at the venue carries no commission. DAC7 (B4c): hosts give their tax details in their area (`host_tax_details`: a person with their date of birth or a business with its registration number, address, tax identification number and issuing country, VAT number, IBAN) and each room's or home's address and land registry number (`booking_resources`); the store sees what is missing, and owners download the year's report (`/admin/{store}/hosts/dac7`, CSV of sellers and of properties) with, per quarter of payment in the store's time zone, what shoppers paid less refunds, the store's commission and the number of bookings, and each home's nights; for the store's own records it also gives Kaizen's fee and the host's net earnings per quarter, which the report itself does not ask for. The store files it (in Norway with Skatteetaten) by 31 January; Kaizen's own fee is not in it, as Kaizen is not treated as the operator. | 2026-09-27 | Defaults chosen for the owner ("use sensible defaults"). Built in parts: B4a (hosts and their area), B4b (payments), B4c (DAC7). Whether Kaizen is itself a platform operator under DAC7 still needs legal advice. |
| D72 | **Phase 2 (AI search and product content) is built in five parts, starting with keyword search in Postgres that needs no AI; each AI part keeps working, as keyword search, when its model is unavailable.** S1 (built): every product translation keeps a text-search document of its title (weighted A) and description (C), stemmed in its own language (`commerce.search_config()`: norwegian, swedish, danish, english and others, `simple` otherwise; a stored generated column `product_translations.search` with a GIN index), and trigram indexes (`pg_trgm` in the `extensions` schema) on titles, SKUs and category and tag names. A search (`src/server/search.ts`) finds active products with a price in the market by their words, by titles close to what was typed (typos, and parts of compound words: "kopp" finds "keramikkopp"), by SKU (an exact SKU first) or by a category or tag of that name, in the market's language where the product has one; titles weigh most. Shoppers search at `/s/{store}/{market}/search` (a search icon in the header; `search` is a reserved page address), with a few products suggested as they type (keyword only, so no model call slows typing; an ARIA combobox, and a plain form without JavaScript); nothing found, the store's top-level categories are offered. Every search on the page (not type-ahead) is logged with its number of results (`search_queries`), kept 90 days because queries can hold personal data, and the store's admin shows searches, the zero-result rate, the most searched and the searches that found nothing (`/admin/{store}/search`). Next: S2 meaning-based search (embeddings in pgvector, fused with keyword results by reciprocal rank fusion), S3 an LLM turning queries into structured filters, with an eval, S4 AI product content (descriptions, translations, attributes, FAQ) approved by staff before publishing, with a filter blocking generic green claims, fake urgency and "best price" claims, and S5 a randomised test of hybrid against keyword search. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner ("start on Phase 2"). Keyword search first because it needs no vendor, gives the baseline the AI parts are measured against, and is what type-ahead and the fallback use anyway. Postgres full-text ranking is not true BM25; a dedicated engine is reconsidered only if relevance or scale needs it. Models will go through Vercel AI Gateway with EU inference and zero data retention (already planned in the residency register); S2 needs the AI Gateway enabled on the Vercel project and pgvector in CI's Postgres image. |
| D73 | **AI providers and models are settings, not code: Kaizen chooses one provider for every store at Platform → AI, and a store's owner may choose their own instead.** A provider is Vercel AI Gateway, Mistral AI, OpenAI, Google Gemini or any other OpenAI-compatible API (a public https address), with an API key (encrypted with `SETTINGS_ENCRYPTION_KEY`, never shown again, only its last four characters), a model for search by meaning (embeddings) and a model for text (understanding searches, product content), either left empty to switch that use off, and the similarity a product needs to be found by meaning. For the gateway, each model can be kept to EU data centres (the request fails rather than run elsewhere) and every request can ask for zero data retention. Kaizen's settings are one row of `commerce.ai_providers` with no store; a store's own row replaces them while on, and switching it off or removing it goes back to Kaizen's. Features ask `aiFor(storeId)` for the connection and call `embedTexts()` or `completeText()` (`src/server/ai.ts`), plain `fetch` to `/embeddings` and `/chat/completions`, and must keep working without AI when there is none or a call fails. Vectors are kept with the name of the model that made them (`embeddingSpace()`), so a new search model re-embeds rather than mixing. Only owners choose a store's AI, as it sends the store's products and shoppers' searches to another company; changes are in the audit log. Test, on both pages, tries both models and shows how similar a matching and an unrelated product come out, which is where the limit belongs. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner ("super easy to change AI provider and model later from the platform side, and store owners may override it with their own"). An OpenAI-compatible API instead of a provider SDK keeps every provider one address away and needs no new package; the AI SDK can still be added for the assistant (Phase 3). No embedding model on the gateway can be pinned to the EU yet (September 2026): Kaizen's planned search model is Mistral's (`mistral/mistral-embed`, served by Mistral from the EU, zero retention), recorded in the residency register. A store's own provider is the store's own processor, under its own agreement. Addresses are limited to public https hosts and redirects are refused, as the server calls them with the key. |
| D74 | **Search by meaning (Phase 2, S2): products are embedded by the store's AI and found by meaning as well as by their words, the two merged by reciprocal rank fusion; without AI, or when it fails, search is by words alone.** Each active product's translations are embedded (`src/server/embeddings.ts`) from their title, categories and tags, and description (at most 4,000 characters) with the store's search model (D73), and kept in `commerce.product_embeddings` (pgvector in the `extensions` schema; no fixed length, as models differ) with the model's `space` and an md5 of what was embedded: a translation is embedded again only when its text, its terms or the model change. The five-minute cron brings every store up to date (Kaizen's AI for stores without their own, then each store's own, at most 256 texts per AI per run, 32 per request), and saving a product does its store at once. A search (`rankedSearch()`) asks for the query's vector (`queryVector()`, cached for days per store, model and text, waiting at most 1.5 seconds), takes the 50 closest products (active, priced in the market, by their closest translation, of the same model) that reach the store's similarity limit, and merges them with the 100 best keyword results by reciprocal rank fusion (k = 60). Type-ahead stays keyword only. Each search's log keeps how close the closest product came (`semantic_best`) and how many results only meaning found (`meaning_results`); the store's Search page shows how often meaning helped, and how close meaning came on searches that found nothing, to set the limit by. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner ("continue with S2"). Reciprocal rank fusion needs no tuning between keyword and vector scores, which are on different scales. An exact scan of a store's own vectors instead of an approximate (HNSW) index: an index across all stores filtered to one can miss that store's products, and stores' catalogues are small; revisit per model when one outgrows it. The table is outside the Drizzle schema, which cannot name a type in another schema. The similarity limit is set per provider after measuring (Test on the AI pages, and the search log). Measured in production with OpenAI's `text-embedding-3-small` (2026-09-27): searches that fit came out at 0.33–0.55 and nonsense at 0.16, so new providers start at 0.3 and Kaizen's was set to 0.3; results by meaning must also come within 0.1 of the closest, so a low limit does not let in everything above it. Searches' vectors and filters are kept in `commerce.search_cache` (shared by every server instance, keyed by an md5 of the model, market, the store's terms for filters, and the search; 30 days, pruned by the five-minute cron): with `'use cache'`, whose memory is per instance, repeated searches in production still waited 3–5 seconds for the model. CI's database is `pgvector/pgvector:pg17`; PGlite loads `@electric-sql/pglite-pgvector`. |
| D75 | **Query understanding (Phase 2, S3): the store's text model reads a search that may hold more than words ("lampe under 500 kr", "billigste notatbok", "hytte for en helg", "på lager") as filters, which drive ordinary SQL; shoppers see what it was read as and can search for their exact words instead.** Only searches of three or more words, or with a digit, a currency or a word about price, order or stock (`worthUnderstanding()`), are sent, so short searches wait for no model. The model is given the store's categories and tags and the market's currency, and answers with JSON (`understandingMessages()`); `cleanFilters()` keeps only the store's own category and tag slugs, known kinds (`goods`, `appointment`, `stay`, `rental`) and orders (`relevance`, `priceLow`, `priceHigh`, `newest`), sane prices turned into minor units in code (a reversed range put right), and, as words to search for, only words the shopper typed. The filters (`filterClause()` in `src/server/search.ts`) keep both keyword and meaning candidates to a category or any below it, a tag, a kind, a variant priced in the range in the market (with VAT, as prices are kept), and in stock (goods with physical stock at an active place, or digital; bookings count as available); a search that is only filters lists what meets them, newest first, and an order asked for sorts the results. Answers are cached per store, text model, market and search, and dropped when the store's categories or tags change (`understandQuery()`); the model is asked at the same time as the search's vector, and a failure or 2.5 seconds without an answer runs the search as typed. The page shows the reading in Kaizen's words (category names, prices formatted in code) with a link to search for exactly what was typed (`exact=1`), and the log keeps the filters (`search_queries.filters`). The eval (`src/lib/query-eval.ts`): 25 searches in Norwegian, Swedish, Danish and English with each reading a good answer may give; any filter a case does not name must be empty, so invented filters fail; a model passes at 90 %, run with "Check query understanding" on Platform → AI or a store's own AI page. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner ("continue with S3"). The model only chooses among what the store has and never states a product, price or stock level (grounding rule): SQL finds the products. Showing the reading and a way out keeps a wrong guess from hiding products. Price filters compare prices with VAT, including for business buyers who see prices without it; revisit if B2B stores need it. Dates ("neste uke", "en helg") are not understood yet: they stay in the words, and the stay or rental picker handles dates. |
| D76 | **AI product texts (Phase 2, S4): in the product editor, staff can ask the store's text model to write a description, improve one, write search texts, or translate the main language's texts into another; the suggestion is shown to edit, is used only when staff copy it in, and is published only when they save the product. It cannot be copied in while the claims filter finds generic environmental claims, false urgency or scarcity, best- or lowest-price claims, amounts of money or stock levels in it.** The panel ("Write with AI", `AiWriter`) shows when the store's AI has a text model (`EditorContext.aiWriting`), beside each language's texts. The model is given only the product's own words from the editor (title, description, search texts, category and tag names, options; never prices, stock or anything outside the product) and told not to add facts, prices, stock, environmental claims, urgency or comparisons, and to write plain text (`writingMessages()`, `src/lib/product-writing.ts`); its answer is cleaned to plain text within each field's limit (`cleanWritten()`). The claims filter (`findClaims()`, `src/lib/claims.ts`) matches whole words with their endings in Norwegian, Swedish, Danish, English and German, and runs again in the browser as staff edit a suggestion. Each suggestion is written to the audit log (`product.ai_suggested`: who, which product, what kind, which model), never its text. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner ("continue with S4"). Human approval by construction: the model never writes to a product, and nothing it suggests is live until staff save. The Empowering Consumers Directive applies from 27 September 2026 and bans generic environmental claims without recognised excellent environmental performance, which a text model cannot know, so the filter blocks them in AI copy rather than warning; it lists, and staff decide (a colour called "grønn" is flagged too). Texts edited and published under the store's editorial responsibility need no AI label (AI Act Art. 50(4)). Attributes, FAQ blocks and review summaries in the plan wait for fields to hold them; the filter is ready for them. |
| D77 | **Search test (Phase 2, S5): platform admins start a randomised test at Platform → Search test; while it runs, each search in every store is given hybrid search (words, meaning and understanding) or keyword search alone at random, and the page compares the two.** Assignment is per search, drawn on the server when the search runs (`drawArm()`, `commerce.search_experiments.keyword_share`, 5–95 %, one test at a time); the keyword arm asks no model. Each search's log row keeps its test and arm (`search_queries.experiment_id`, `arm`), and its result links go through `/s/{store}/{market}/search/go`, which records which result of which search was opened (`search_clicks`, with its place in the list) and opens the product; the links are plain `rel="nofollow"` links, never prefetched, and results pages ask crawlers not to follow them. The results (`experimentResults()`, arithmetic in `src/lib/experiment-stats.ts`) compare the arms on the share of searches with results where one was opened (primary) and the share that found nothing, each with a 95 % interval for the difference, the average place of the first opened result, and week by week. Decision rule fixed in advance (`verdict()`): nothing is read before 200 searches in each arm; hybrid is better or worse only when the whole interval is on one side; a sample-ratio check (chi-square against the share asked for) below p = 0.001 marks the results as broken. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner ("continue with S5"). No cookie or identifier: the plan leaves using the cart or session token for experiments to counsel, so the unit is the search, not the shopper, and the result link records opening without storing anything in the browser. That measures search quality (the plan's upstream metrics for a launch-stage store), not revenue per searcher, which needs a shopper-level identifier; once counsel allows one, the arm can be drawn per shopper and joined to carts and orders. A shopper can meet both arms in one visit. Clicks go with their search after 90 days. |
| D78 | **Sorting and filtering product listings: a "Filter and sort" dialog on the products page (new, `/s/{store}/{market}/products`, a menu link kind "All products"), category and tag pages and search results, over every kind of product.** The choices are the page's address (`src/lib/listing-filters.ts`: `kind`, `category`, `tag`, `o.{option}` for variant options, `min`/`max` in whole units, `stock=1`, `sort`), applied on the server (`listingProducts()` in `src/server/listing.ts`) and shown above the list, each with a link that takes it away. The dialog (`FilterDialog`) offers only what the page's products have and would narrow them, with counts (`listingFacets()`): kinds, categories (with those below them), tags as features, each variant option by name (size, colour, a rental's period …; any value of an option, every option on the same variant) and the price range. Prices are compared as the shopper is shown them: without VAT for a business (`shownPrice()`), and a store selling to both lists each kind of buyer only their products. Sorts: featured (the page's own order, search relevance for results), newest, price either way, name. Category, tag and search conditions are shared with search's filters (`src/server/product-conditions.ts`). | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner. Filters in the address make a filtered list shareable and work with the back button, and keep the page's own list prerendered: listing pages show it as the fallback while the filtered list streams in per request, so they stay fast and readable without script, and canonical addresses point at the unfiltered page. Categories and tags are the only "features" the catalogue has; `products.attributes` is unused, and variant options are free text per product, so options are matched by name and value as typed. Counts are over the page's products before filters, not after, to keep one cached query per page. `products` is now a reserved store page address. |
| D79 | **Product layouts: a store lays out its product pages in the page builder, with product components for the product's parts, and chooses where each layout is used: for single products, for product categories and tags, or as the store's standard.** The product page's parts are one block kind, `product` with a `part` (`PRODUCT_PARTS`: back link, pictures, title with the wishlist heart, price, business-only notice, host, buy, description, right of withdrawal, product safety, and related products, new), each with only its own settings (`ProductBlock`) and defaults that draw the page as before; `ProductPartView` (`src/components/product-parts.tsx`) draws them with the product, the buy part adapting to its kind (goods with stock and subscriptions, appointments' times, stays' and rentals' dates), and a part with nothing for the product leaves no space. Layouts are pages of type `product_layout` (a store's only, no address, categories or tags; product components are refused anywhere else), edited under Product layouts with the same editor, whose palette then offers the product's parts as sketches on the canvas; a layout's preview shows it with any of the store's products. `productLayoutFor()` picks the product's own layout, else its nearest category's (a category's counts for those below it), else a tag's, else the store's standard (`stores`, `terms` and `products.product_layout_id`, checked to be the store's own layouts, let go when one is deleted), and only published ones; without one, the built-in `DEFAULT_PRODUCT_LAYOUT`, the page as it was. The page's structured data is drawn once, outside the layout. | 2026-09-27 | Asked for by the owner. One block kind with parts keeps the page builder's many per-kind lists to one entry; the parts are the page's existing pieces moved, so the standard layout is the page shoppers had (the browser tests pass unchanged). Layouts are pages so drafts, publishing, translations, saved parts and copying to new stores come with them; assignments are columns rather than a table so the database keeps them to the store's own layouts and lets go on delete, as with front pages (D54). `clone_store()` copies layouts as it copies published pages, and their uses. A category's layout counting for subcategories follows how category listings include them (D50). |
| D80 | **The site's header and footer are built in the page builder, from site components, for Kaizen and for each store; a header can lie over the top of pages that start with a background: every page, the front page, or pages in chosen page categories or tags.** The header's and footer's pieces are one block kind, `site` with a `part` (`SITE_PARTS`: logo, menu, phone menu button, search, account or sign in, wishlist, cart, countries, business or private switch, Start your store, business details, cookies link), each with only its own settings (`SiteBlock`: the logo's height, which menu and whether its links go side by side, the countries as a list to open or as links, hidden on phones); `SitePartView` (`src/components/site-parts.tsx`) draws them with the store in its country, or with Kaizen's settings, from the same pieces as the standard header and footer. Headers and footers are pages of type `header` and `footer` (no address, categories or tags; site components are refused anywhere else, and a footer must keep the business details and the cookies link), edited under Headers and Footers with the same editor; new ones start as the standard ones (`defaultHeader()`, `defaultFooter()`). The site uses the one chosen on its list (`stores.header_id`/`footer_id`, `platform_settings.header_id`/`footer_id`, checked to be the owner's own of that type, let go when deleted), as last published; without one, the standard header and footer as before. For headers the builder gains three general options: row layouts whose side columns fit their content (`fit-sides`, `fit-middle`, `fit-end`), rows that keep their columns side by side on phones, and columns whose components sit side by side. A header's `overlay` says where it lies over the page; a page the rule covers and whose first row has a background marks itself (`data-header-overlay`, `headerOverlays()`), and the stylesheet then takes the header out of the flow, see-through with its own text colour while the page is at its top, and with its background again once scrolled. The same pattern as product layouts (D79): one block kind with parts, layouts as pages so drafts, publishing, translations, previews and copying to new stores come with them, and the choice as columns the database keeps to the owner's own. Keeping the standard header and footer until one is chosen changes nothing for sites that do not use it; the browser tests also pass with the standard layouts chosen. The overlay is only over a first row with a background, since that is what it is for and it would otherwise cover the page's first words; pages mark themselves so the rule follows client navigation with no knowledge of addresses in the layout. A footer must keep the business details and cookies link, as the law and the cookie choices (D58) ask. |
| D81 | **Kaizen and every store can have an AI chat agent on their pages, typed or spoken, that answers only about the site from its published pages, articles, products and a knowledge base of the owner's documents, and opens pages for the visitor as it talks.** The owner gives it a name, an occupation, a picture, a greeting per language, guidance on tone, a daily message limit and whether visitors may talk (`commerce.chat_agents`, one per site; `/admin/{store}/chat`, `/admin/platform/chat`). It uses the site's AI (D73): a text model with tools (`chatWithTools()`), and for voice a speech-to-text model, a text-to-speech model and a voice (new columns on `ai_providers`); a recording is written down, answered like a typed message and read out (push to talk, `/api/chat/voice`), not a live audio session. The tools are the site's own reads: product search (D72, D74) and one product's variants, prices and stock, passages from the knowledge (`knowledge_chunks`: the site's published pages and articles in each language, cut again within five minutes of publishing, and the knowledge base's documents, pasted or read from text, Markdown, PDF or Word files, found by keyword and meaning merged by reciprocal rank), the site's facts (contact details, countries, shipping), and navigate, which only opens the site's own pages that exist (a product, page, article, category or tag by its address, a search, the cart, account, wishlist). Conversations are kept only in the visitor's tab (`kaizen_chat` in sessionStorage, necessary) and sent with each message; nothing is stored on the server but counts: 30 messages per visitor per ten minutes (a daily-changing hash of the address) and the site's daily limit (`chat_usage`, pruned after two days). Push to talk with the site's configured models keeps voice on the same provider choice, key and EU settings as everything else (D73) and works with any OpenAI-compatible API, where a realtime audio session would tie the chat to one provider. Keeping conversations in the visitor's tab keeps no personal data on the server. The AI Act asks that people are told they talk to an AI. |

A link keeps its item's depth under the ones before it, so a menu is a flat list in order (`MenuEntry.depth`) made a tree where it is drawn (`menuTree()`); a link to something not published or gone is left out, the links under it taking its place. The editor knows nothing of places (WordPress's Menu settings are left out): the standard header (and the phone's slide-out menu) and footer show the menus chosen under Header and footer (`header_menu_id`, `footer_menu_id`, which let go when the menu is deleted), and a Menu component in any page, header or footer shows the one it names, side by side (links under a link in a list opening below it on hover or keyboard focus, no script) or one under another. The editor lists where each menu is used. Lists that open on hover and focus need no script, so prerendered pages keep working without it. |

| D87 | **A top link in a menu can be a mega menu: the links under it open side by side across the header's width, in columns, each with a picture of its own and the links under it.** Set on the link in the menu editor (`MenuEntry.mega`: 1–`MEGA_MAX_COLUMNS` columns, optionally centred); the links right under it take an uploaded picture (`MenuEntry.image`), shown above them. | D88 | **Each site has a media library of every picture and video it uploaded, anywhere in the admin, with where each is used.** `commerce.media` keeps one row per file (the store's, or Kaizen's with a null store): its address and small copy, Storage bucket and paths, name, type, size, width and height (read from the file's first bytes on upload, `imageSize()`, else measured by the browser when shown) and a description. The library (`/admin/{store}/media`, `/admin/platform/media`) adds files in bulk, and searches as the storefront does: keyword (the name's words and the description, with prefixes and trigrams) and, with the site's AI, meaning (`media_embeddings`, the name, description and uses embedded in the five-minute cron), merged by reciprocal rank fusion. | D89 | **The media library's pictures have alt texts in each of the site's languages, written by staff or by the site's AI looking at the picture, and the site shows them wherever a picture has none of its own.** `media.alt` is the owner's main language's (a store's first market's, English for Kaizen), `alt_translations` the others by locale, `alt_source` who wrote them (`ai` or `staff`; the AI never writes over staff's unless someone asks on that picture). The site uses them wherever a picture has no alt text of its own: products, their variants and the cart through `commerce.media_alt(url, locale)` (by either address, the market's language else the main one), pages, articles, headers, footers and product layouts through `withLibraryAlts()` in their loaders. Saved without review because the owner asked for it and the texts are short, grounded in the picture and filtered for claims; they are marked as the AI's and can be changed at any time. |
| D90 | **The media library searches a file's name by name alone, finds pictures by their alt texts in every language, and always has English alt texts; files list as a grid or a list.** A search that looks like a file's name (`isFileNameQuery()`: a file type's ending, or one word of letters and digits such as a stored file's id) finds that file, then names starting with or holding it, and never searches by meaning or likeness. Vectors are made from a file's alt texts in every language, its name only where it is words (`readableFileName()`: no ids or numbers) and what uses it; a file with none of that has no vector, and fuzzy name matching ignores ids.
